Superseded by the fix below; `GATEWAY_ALERT_RESET_AFTER_QUIET_CYCLES` was removed again as a result (no longer needed).
  - **Current implementation: a fixed, one-time TTL of exactly `GATEWAY_ALERT_NOTIFY_COOLDOWN_SECONDS` (24h by default), applied once per fresh window and never extended afterwards - a guaranteed ceiling regardless of how much more data arrives in between.**
    - `record_gateway_alert_occurrence()`: `INCR`s `_COUNT_KEY` as before, then checks `TTL(_COUNT_KEY) < 0` (i.e. the key exists with no expiry yet - true only the first time a fresh window's counter is created) and only then applies `EXPIRE`. Every subsequent occurrence within that same window still increments the counter but does **not** touch its TTL - so the counter always reverts to 0 exactly `GATEWAY_ALERT_NOTIFY_COOLDOWN_SECONDS` after the window's first occurrence, full stop.
    - `mark_gateway_alert_notified()`: sets `_LAST_NOTIFIED_KEY` with `SET ... EX=GATEWAY_ALERT_NOTIFY_COOLDOWN_SECONDS` directly on every successful notification - since this only ever runs once per notification (not once per occurrence), the TTL is inherently non-renewed already; the write itself simply carries the same fixed window.
    - `should_notify_gateway_alert()`'s own time-diff comparison is left in place, unchanged, as a secondary/clock-skew guard - now largely redundant in practice since the TTL enforces the identical window on its own, but kept rather than removed since it costs nothing and doesn't rely on Redis's clock matching the application's.
  - **Why a fixed TTL over a background thread/scheduler:** still avoids adding any new periodic task, timer thread, or polling loop to an otherwise purely event-driven consumer - the reset is a side effect Redis performs on its own on a fixed schedule, not something this module has to actively track or a background job has to sweep for.
  - This deliberately changes the occurrence counter's semantics from a pure lifetime tally (its original description) to "occurrences within the current fixed 24h window" - an explicit, intentional trade-off per this instruction, not an oversight.

- [x] **`gateway_recover` now consumed to reset the throttle early, superseding the need to wait out the fixed-TTL window for the common case - the TTL fallback above is retained, not removed.**
  - **Context: the original objection to this no longer applies.** The earlier "self-resetting counter/cooldown" item above exists specifically because an earlier attempt at "clear the cooldown when the gateway recovers" required a new cross-service event pushed by `telegram_gateway`, and the user's explicit instruction at the time was not to touch `telegram_gateway` for this. Since then, `telegram_gateway` gained a `gateway_recover` event (`type: "gateway_recover"`, `task_id`/`session_id` both `null`, fired once per incident after a prior `gateway_alert`) for its own, unrelated reason - closing its own CCR-019 finding, nothing to do with `bot_sanctuary`. **Consuming it here requires zero `telegram_gateway` changes** - it was already being published, `bot_sanctuary` just wasn't listening for it (falling into `_dispatch_to_session()` and being dropped as a "missing `session_id`" message instead, silently, before this fix).
  - **Resets both `_COUNT_KEY` and `_LAST_NOTIFIED_KEY`, not just the cooldown** - per explicit instruction. `telegram_gateway` only ever emits `gateway_recover` after a real prior `gateway_alert` for that same incident, so receiving it is an unambiguous "that incident is over" signal - the next occurrence is treated as a brand new, unrelated incident: fresh count from 0, immediately eligible to notify, rather than merely allowed to notify earlier than the cooldown would have permitted on its own.
  - **The fixed 24h TTL fallback (above) is deliberately left completely unchanged, not superseded.** Per explicit instruction ("Redis 24 hours expiry still hold"). `reset_gateway_alert_throttle()` is an earlier, precise, event-driven reset for the common case where the `gateway_recover` message is actually received; the TTL remains the guaranteed ceiling for the case it isn't (`bot_sanctuary` down at the moment it was published, RabbitMQ trouble, or the message dropped after exhausting `Q_CONSUME_MAX_ATTEMPTS` on this end) - defence in depth, not redundant.
  - **No extra guard before resetting** (e.g. re-checking `should_notify_gateway_alert()` first) - considered and judged unnecessary, since `gateway_recover`'s own "once per incident, only after a real alert" semantics already guarantee a reset here is always warranted.
  - `message_handler.py`: new `_handle_gateway_recover()`, dispatched via a new `elif message_type == "gateway_recover":` branch in `process_message()` - added explicitly alongside `gateway_alert`/`session_cleared` (not left to fall through to `_dispatch_to_session()`), since it carries the same `session_id: null` shape and would otherwise be logged as an errored/dropped session message instead of the routine recovery signal it actually is.
  - `utils_redis/database.py`: new `reset_gateway_alert_throttle()` - `DEL`s both keys, same fail-open/non-fatal try/except convention as every other function in this module (a failed reset just leaves the TTL fallback to handle it on schedule, not a correctness risk).
  - `README.md`: flow diagram and throttle-window note updated to show the `gateway_recover` branch and its reset effect.

- [x] **Per-call Redis retry added, bringing this module to parity with `telegram_gateway`'s own `_redis_write()`/`_redis_read()`/`_redis_delete()` convention.**
  - **Context:** every function in this module (`record_gateway_alert_occurrence()`, `should_notify_gateway_alert()`, `mark_gateway_alert_notified()`, `reset_gateway_alert_throttle()`) made exactly one attempt at its Redis call, catching any exception and falling straight to that function's existing fail-open/non-fatal behaviour - a real gap compared to `telegram_gateway`, which retries every one of its own equivalent primitives up to `REDIS_TASK_MAX_ATTEMPTS` times before giving up the same way.
  - **New `REDIS_TASK_MAX_ATTEMPTS`/`REDIS_TASK_RETRY_DELAY` settings added (`config.py`), same names/defaults (5 attempts, 1s apart) as `telegram_gateway`'s own** - deliberate parity, not a coincidence, so the two applications' Redis retry behaviour is tunable the same way if ever needed.
  - **No function's return contract, fail-open policy, or non-fatal-on-failure behaviour changed** - retry only replaces "one attempt, then give up" with "up to `REDIS_TASK_MAX_ATTEMPTS` attempts, then give up exactly the same way as before." `should_notify_gateway_alert()` still fails open (`True`) on exhaustion; `record_gateway_alert_occurrence()` still returns `None`; `mark_gateway_alert_notified()`/`reset_gateway_alert_throttle()` still log-and-continue, non-fatal.
  - **`_get_redis_client()`'s own single-attempt lazy-connect is deliberately left untouched** - out of scope for this change, which is about retrying an already-established connection's individual command, not initial connection setup (mirrors `telegram_gateway`'s own `_redis_write()`/etc. retry scope, which likewise doesn't touch `initialise_redis_connection()`).
  - **Scope explicitly excludes `RabbitMQPublisher.publish()`/the RabbitMQ consume path** - raised in the same conversation as a possible second gap, audited, and found not to be the same class of problem: `RabbitMQPublisher` is a per-instance, never-shared connection by design (unlike `telegram_gateway`'s shared module-level publish channel that CCR-021 fixed), already retries internally, and already resets its own connection state more explicitly on failure than `telegram_gateway`'s equivalent does. **Explicit user decision: left untouched** - no lock or further change applied there, pending confirmation of a concrete gap.

- [ ] **Not yet done:** non-blocking dispatch - `send_mail()` (and now the Redis throttle calls) still run synchronously on the RabbitMQ consumer thread inside `_handle_gateway_alert()`, so a slow/hanging SMTP or Redis call can stall message consumption. Not a concern for `gateway_alert` itself (rare, systemic events), but worth revisiting once the `SessionWorker` pipeline (§3) exists, since the same consumer thread will by then also be responsible for dispatching every session's work - any blocking call here would delay that too.
- [ ] Reuses the SMTP alerting groundwork already specified in `bot_orchestrator/CODE_TODO.md` item 1 (`utils_email/email_handler.py`, throttling, non-blocking dispatch) — historical reference only; `bot_orchestrator` no longer exists in this repository, superseded by the implementation above.

## 5. Agent "Call" model

Status: **Phase 1 (scaffolding) and Phase 2 (LLM + persona wiring for one Call) done.** `utilities/utils_calls/` exists with a real LLM binding per Call; only the entry Call (Chat, persona "Rukia") has an actual system prompt written - the other four still just echo whatever `query_llm()` returns with no persona prepended. See the phased plan at the end of this section for what's still open; nothing past Phase 2 is built.

- [x] **New: `utilities/utils_calls/` created — directory-per-domain convention, same as `utils_agents/`/`utils_queue/`/etc.** Naming: `utils_calls`, not the originally-suggested `utils_type_handler` — proposed as a better fit for this project's existing `utils_<domain>` convention (names the domain - the "Call" model itself - not the mechanism), adopted without objection.
  - **One file per Call**, `<name>_call.py` (`chat_call.py`, `architect_call.py`, `coder_call.py`, `review_call.py`, `documentation_call.py`), same one-file-per-provider shape `utils_agents/` already uses for LLM providers. Each exports a `CALL_NAME` constant and a single `async def handle(prompt: str) -> str | None`. Each module's own Notes record what it's still missing (persona for four of the five, handoff decision logic, and - for the four non-Chat Calls - the tool/filesystem access §6 already commits to but has no mechanism decided for yet).
  - **New `call_router.py` — the "function to move call type from one to another", per explicit instruction.** `handoff_call(target_call, prompt)` looks up any of the five Calls by name and dispatches a prompt to it — deliberately **any-to-any**, not a fixed pipeline order, matching this section's own "not a strict one-way pipeline" requirement below. `get_call(call_name)` is the underlying registry lookup, mirroring `utils_agents/agent_interface.py::_resolve_provider()`'s own shape.
  - **Deliberate design choice, worth flagging explicitly: individual Call modules do not import each other or `call_router.py`, and `call_router.py` is the only module that imports all five.** The alternative — every Call module importing every other Call module (and/or the router) so it can "call" another Call directly — would create a circular-import mesh across five files for no real benefit. Instead, a Call module is meant to stay self-contained (prompt in, reply out); *deciding* whether to hand off, and to whom, is intended to live in the per-thread turn loop that calls `handoff_call()` (Phase 3 below), not inside a Call module itself. This still satisfies "all call types can call one another" — `handoff_call()` accepts any target, so every Call is reachable from every other via the router — just centralised rather than peer-to-peer. **Not yet confirmed this is what "call one another" meant** — flagged in case a more literal peer-to-peer shape was actually wanted; the peer-to-peer version is a strictly bigger, more tightly-coupled change (five modules needing awareness of each other) and was not built without confirming first.
  - **Renamed: `rukia_call.py` → `chat_call.py`, per explicit instruction, all dependencies updated.** `call_router.py`'s import and registry entry, every doc/comment reference (`CODE_TODO.md`, module Notes across `utils_calls/`), and the named-role list below all updated from "Rukia Call" to "Chat Call" — Rukia is now the *persona* this Call's system prompt describes (`chat_call.py::PERSONA_PROMPT`), not the Call's own identifier (`CALL_NAME = "chat"`), the same relationship "claude"/"codex" (provider identifiers) already have to whatever the underlying assistant calls itself. The rename itself was done directly on disk (by the user) before this instruction arrived - this entry covers updating every *dependency* on the old name/module, not the file move itself.
- [x] **Phase 2 done for the Chat Call only, per explicit instruction ("implement only phase 2").** `config.py` gained `LLM_ARCHITECT_TYPE`/`LLM_CODER_TYPE`/`LLM_REVIEW_TYPE`/`LLM_DOCUMENTATION_TYPE`, each falling back to `LLM_CHAT_TYPE` if unset (resolved once at startup, in `config.py` itself, not per-call) - this is what actually revisits §2's "on hold" per-Call routing item, now that a concrete Call exists to attach it to. Every one of the five `<name>_call.py::handle()` functions now calls `utils_agents/agent_interface.py::query_llm()` with its own resolved `llm_type`.
  - **`chat_call.py::PERSONA_PROMPT` is the user-authored "Rukia" persona, stored verbatim** - a detailed character/tone specification (calm, quietly introverted, technology-enthusiast, sparing emote use, web-search-only tool access). Not rewritten or edited by this change - stored exactly as given.
  - **`PERSONA_PROMPT = ""` for architect_call.py/coder_call.py/review_call.py/documentation_call.py, per explicit instruction ("leave coder, reviewer, and documentation's persona empty for now").** LLM wiring is real for all four; persona text is not. Architect wasn't explicitly named in that instruction alongside coder/reviewer/documentation - treated the same way here for consistency (all four non-Chat Calls left equally unwritten) rather than left on the pre-Phase-2 placeholder shape - **flagged in case Architect was actually meant to be excluded from this pass**, since it wasn't named directly.
  - **User-reported observation, recorded rather than acted on: the Rukia persona's actual responses have come across as "very cold rather than an introvert."** The persona text itself was not modified in response to this - it's stored as authored. Left as an open item below for the user to decide whether/how to iterate on the wording (e.g. loosening a constraint that may be reading as terse rather than "quietly warm"), rather than unilaterally rewriting an authored creative asset.
  - **Deployment plumbing kept in sync**, same files every previous `LLM_*` addition touched: `compose.dev.yml`, `bot_sanctuary/setup.sh`'s `bs_dev_run_docker()` (exposed branch), root `setup.sh`'s `get_masked_config_variables()`, `config_sample.ini`. Four new `CHATBOT_LLM_<CALL>_TYPE` variables, one per non-Chat Call (Chat itself reuses the already-existing `CHATBOT_LLM_CHAT_TYPE`).
  - **Revised, same day: persona delivery switched from plain string concatenation to each provider's own native mechanism, per explicit instruction ("I want an agent instead of a persona. This effectively cost more tokens than required instead of using what is pre-existing").** The original Phase 2 shape (`f"{PERSONA_PROMPT}\n\n{prompt}"`, one combined string sent to `query_llm()`) re-sent the entire persona as ordinary prompt tokens on every single call, indistinguishable from user content, with no eligibility for any provider's own prompt caching. Replaced with an explicit `persona` argument threaded through `query_llm()` → each `<provider>_interface.py::query_via_oauth()/query_via_api()`, delivered natively per provider:
    - **Claude** — `ClaudeAgentOptions(system_prompt=persona)` passed to `claude_agent_sdk.query()`. Deliberately the plain `system_prompt` option, not the SDK's separate `agents={name: AgentDefinition(...)}` subagent mechanism (despite `PERSONA_PROMPT`'s own text already being written in that frontmatter shape - `name:`/`description:`/`tools:`/`model:`) - subagents are for on-demand specialised helpers a conversation delegates to mid-turn, whereas a Call's persona is meant to be in effect for the entire call, which `system_prompt` matches directly. Also the option that benefits from Anthropic's own prompt caching across repeated calls with the same persona - the "costs more tokens than required" complaint's direct fix for this provider.
    - **Codex** — an `AGENTS.md` file, codex's own pre-existing convention for persistent instructions read automatically at session start. To avoid a shared-state race if two concurrent calls ever used different personas, each call with a persona gets its own throwaway temp working directory containing just that call's `AGENTS.md`, `codex exec` is run with `cwd` set to it, and the directory is deleted again once the call finishes - rather than writing to one shared path (e.g. the bind-mounted `~/.codex/AGENTS.md`).
    - **DeepSeek/Qwen** — a leading `{"role": "system", "content": persona}` message ahead of the user message, the standard OpenAI-compatible convention these two REST APIs already support.
    - **Not yet revisited as part of this change:** whether this measurably improves the "cold rather than introverted" tone complaint above - a real system-prompt channel typically produces stronger persona adherence than one concatenated block of text, but this hasn't been tested against a live response yet.
  - **Revised again, same day: persona content moved out of Python entirely, into a new `bot_sanctuary_application/libraries/` folder, per explicit instruction ("I want each agent optimised for each LLM, in such the persona for call is meant for claude agent with tools and model spelled out").** This confirmed a flag raised in the previous revision - `PERSONA_PROMPT`'s `tools:`/`model:` frontmatter really was written specifically for a Claude agent, not a generic persona meant to be reused verbatim across all four providers - and that a Call's persona should be genuinely per-provider content, not one shared string.
    - **`libraries/<provider>/<call_name>.md` - one file per provider (`claude`/`codex`/`deepseek`/`qwen`) per Call (`chat`/`architect`/`coder`/`review`/`documentation`), 20 files total**, mirroring `utils_calls/`'s one-file-per-Call convention and `bot_directory`'s one-folder-per-provider convention. Pure content, no Python - matches `bot_directory`'s own "content, not code" role. Only `libraries/claude/chat.md` has real content (the user-authored Rukia persona, moved verbatim, now written with proper `---`-delimited YAML frontmatter and Markdown body rather than the flatter plain-text shape it started as - **this reformatting was done directly on disk by the user after the file was created, not by this change**); the remaining 19 are empty placeholders, consistent with `architect_call.py`/`coder_call.py`/`review_call.py`/`documentation_call.py`'s own empty `PERSONA_PROMPT` treatment.
    - **`utils_agents/agent_interface.py::load_persona(llm_type, call_name)`** reads `libraries/<llm_type>/<call_name>.md`, returns `None` if the file is missing or empty/whitespace-only. Every `<name>_call.py::handle()` now calls this itself (using its own already-resolved `LLM_<CALL>_TYPE`) instead of holding a `PERSONA_PROMPT` constant - so switching a Call's configured provider automatically picks up that provider's own persona file on the next call, no restart needed.
    - **`claude_interface.py::_parse_persona()`** splits the loaded content's `---`-delimited frontmatter (`name:`/`description:`/`tools:`/`model:`) from its Markdown body, and `_run_query()` wires the pieces into real `ClaudeAgentOptions` fields - `system_prompt=body`, `tools=<parsed list>`, `model=<parsed value>` - rather than the previous revision's `ClaudeAgentOptions(system_prompt=persona)` (whole string, frontmatter included as inert prose). `name:`/`description:` are recognised and stripped from the body but don't map to any `ClaudeAgentOptions` field today - kept purely as authoring metadata, matching Claude Code's own subagent-file convention. Malformed frontmatter (an opening `---` with no closing one) falls back to treating the whole content as body, rather than guessing.
    - **Codex/DeepSeek/Qwen interfaces are unchanged by this revision** - they already took `persona` as a plain string (an `AGENTS.md` file for Codex; a `system` role message for DeepSeek/Qwen), which is exactly what a provider-specific library file for them is expected to contain too (no frontmatter needed, since none of the three have an equivalent structured-config concept the way Claude's `AgentDefinition`/`ClaudeAgentOptions` do).
    - **Open, not yet decided:** whether Codex's/DeepSeek's/Qwen's own library files should ever grow their own lightweight metadata convention (e.g. Codex's own model-selection flags, if `codex exec` exposes any), or stay plain-text personas indefinitely - no immediate need, since none of the three have unwired "spelled-out" fields the way Claude's `tools:`/`model:` did.
- [ ] Named roles: **Chat Call** (entry/default, conversation & routing only, persona "Rukia" - see `chat_call.py`), **Architect Call**, **Coder Call**, **Review Call**, **Documentation Call**. (LLM-wired above - handoff/turn-loop/tool-access/whitelist still not.)
- [ ] Any Call may respond directly or hand off to another Call. (`call_router.py::handoff_call()` provides the mechanism - nothing yet decides *when* to use it.)
- [ ] Per-thread main loop: if the active Call doesn't resolve the task, hand off to the next Call; that Call can hand back — a bounded back-and-forth ("agent call"), not a strict one-way pipeline. (Not yet built - see Phase 3/4 below.)
- [ ] **Corrected — whitelist enforcement moved to `telegram_gateway`, not owned here.** `telegram_gateway` is the only component that actually knows chat_id identity, and it already has the exact precedent pattern (`SESSION_RESET_ALLOWED_CHAT_IDS`, same comma-separated-env-var → `set[int]` style). `bot_sanctuary` does **not** read an env var or perform its own lookup — it trusts an access-tier field stamped onto the inbound task payload by `telegram_gateway` (see follow-up item added to `telegram_gateway/CODE_TODO.md`).
  - A non-whitelisted user's task arrives tagged Chat-only — no handoff, no access to Architect/Coder/Review/Documentation, effectively a single-agent conversational experience with no repository access (see section 6).
  - A whitelisted user's task arrives tagged for the full Call-handoff graph.
  - **Note the behavioural difference from `SESSION_RESET_ALLOWED_CHAT_IDS`'s pattern:** that whitelist silently drops a disallowed request entirely (log only, no action). This one does not reject anything — the task still proceeds normally, just scoped to Chat only. It's a tagging/tiering mechanism, not a gate.
- [ ] Each agent gets a defined tool set with explicit specs, so the model can call them directly with no extra glue per tool.
  - **New (2026-09-12): `utils_agents/agent_tools.py` - the reply-side half of this item now built, ahead of Phase 5, per explicit instruction to compile telegram_gateway's `message_handler.py` message types into agent-callable tools.** This is deliberately scoped to *outbound reply* tools only (how a Call turns its own decision into a message telegram_gateway will accept) - it is not the §6 filesystem/code tool access Architect/Coder/Review/Documentation still need; those remain a separate, still-undesigned mechanism under Phase 5 below.
    - **One tool per type `telegram_gateway`'s `utils_queue/message_handler.py::process_message()` dispatches on the agent -> gateway side**: `poll`/`image`/`video`/`album`/`file`/`text`/`completed`/`error`. `session_reset`/`bot_started` are deliberately excluded, not an oversight - both are orchestrator/lifecycle-authority actions (see `telegram_gateway/README.md`'s "Execution authority... belongs to the orchestrator, not this gateway"), already exclusively triggered by the whitelisted admin command/optional daily schedule this application's own `utils_session/session_worker.py` implements - exposing either as something an LLM can call on a whim would let a Call trigger a global session wipe itself, which nothing in this project's design permits.
    - **`get_tool_definitions()`** - returns a deep copy of a provider-agnostic tool table (`name`/`description`/`parameters`, JSON-schema-style), one entry per tool above. Deliberately not yet translated into any specific provider's own tool-calling request shape (Claude's `tool_use` blocks vs. an OpenAI-compatible `functions` array, relevant once DeepSeek/Qwen are ever wired for tool-calling too) - left to whichever `<provider>_interface.py` actually wires real tool-calling in Phase 5 below, since that translation is a provider concern, not a tool-definition concern.
    - **`validate_tool_arguments(tool_name, arguments)`** - checks a tool call's arguments against its payload shape and returns `(payload, None)` if valid (`payload` still missing `task_id`/`session_id`) or `(None, corrective_message)` if not - an unrecognised `tool_name` is itself a rejection, listing the recognised set. **`execute_tool(publisher, task_id, session_id, tool_name, arguments)`** - the actual "if valid, allow the message to be pushed into queue... if not valid, respond back to the agent on the correct format" entry point requested: validates first, and only on success builds the full envelope (`task_id`/`session_id` added here, never accepted as tool arguments - mirrors `telegram_gateway`'s own "identity-blind agents" principle applied to the outbound side) and publishes it via the caller's own `RabbitMQPublisher` (a `SessionWorker`'s long-lived instance, or a disposable one - never shared across threads, same constraint `utils_queue/queue.py`'s own module header already documents). Returns `None` on a successful publish; otherwise the corrective message (a validation failure, or a publish failure) - designed to be handed straight back to the calling agent as the tool's own result, so it can retry with a corrected call.
    - **Validation strictness, a deliberate, flagged departure from a pure 1:1 mirror of `message_handler.py`:** most checks mirror that file's own drop conditions exactly (`album`'s item shape is a direct copy of its `_is_valid_album_item()`). A few are stricter than its bare minimum - requiring at least one `poll` option, a non-empty `image`/`video`/`file` `url`, non-empty `text` - each covering a case `message_handler.py` itself doesn't check at all and would otherwise forward straight to Telegram for a guaranteed rejection. Reasoning: catching it here means the calling agent learns the correct format immediately, rather than the call round-tripping through RabbitMQ only to be silently dropped, or - for a send that actually reaches Telegram - reported back later as a `delivery_failed` event (see that README's Tier 1/Tier 2 split). Deliberately **not** further strengthened against `telegram_gateway`'s own README-documented Telegram API limits (poll question/option length caps, message/caption length caps, inline keyboard size limits) - those are `telegram_gateway`-side, environment-configurable constants (`TELEGRAM_MESSAGE_MAX_LENGTH` etc.); hardcoding a duplicate copy of Telegram's public defaults here would silently drift the moment an operator changes the real value on `telegram_gateway`'s side, so those cases are deliberately left to that existing Tier 1 `delivery_failed` reporting path rather than guessed at redundantly here.
    - **Update (2026-09-12): `execute_tool()` is now wired to a real caller - see Phase 4 below.** `get_tool_definitions()` is still unconsumed - nothing yet hands this tool table to an LLM for it to decide between; the Chat Call's reply is still deposited into the `"text"` tool by code (`utils_calls/turn_handler.py::run_turn_and_reply()`), not chosen by the LLM itself via real function-calling. That remains Phase 5's job.
- [ ] **Open, user-reported:** the Rukia persona (`chat_call.py::PERSONA_PROMPT`) reads "cold" rather than "quietly introverted/warm" in actual responses - not yet diagnosed or iterated on. Possible angles for later: the "Avoid excessive punctuation/enthusiasm" instructions may be overpowering the "warm"/"gentle" instructions in practice, or this may be a prompt-engineering tuning pass rather than a structural issue at all.

### Phased plan for the rest of this section (Phase 2 done for Chat only, Phase 4 minimally done Chat-only)

- [x] **Phase 1 — scaffolding.** `utils_calls/` created: one placeholder module per Call, a central `call_router.py` with any-to-any `handoff_call()`.
- [x] **Phase 2 — LLM + persona, Chat Call only.** Per-Call `LLM_<CALL>_TYPE` settings added (`config.py`); every Call's `handle()` now calls `query_llm()` for real. Only Chat has an actual persona - Architect/Coder/Review/Documentation still have `PERSONA_PROMPT = ""`, per explicit instruction, to be filled in later.
- [x] **Phase 3 — handoff decision logic, mechanism implemented 2026-09-12, per explicit instruction ("use call_request to rotate between call_types, if call_request do not exist, push it to tools_check"). Mechanism only - unexercised beyond Chat, see caveats below.** The signal a Call returns to request a handoff is a plain `{"call_request": "<call_name>"}` dict - simpler than the originally-proposed `CallResult(reply, handoff_to)` shape, and no Call imports `call_router` itself (still true - `turn_handler.py` is the only caller of `call_router.get_call()`, keeping Phase 1's "no cross-Call imports" design intact). `utils_calls/turn_handler.py::run_turn()` now loops (bounded by new `_MAX_CALL_HOPS = 5`), starting at `"chat"`: resolves the current call_name via `call_router.get_call()`, calls its `handle(prompt)`, and either rotates to the named Call (`"call_request"` present) or treats the result as the final tool message to hand to `tools_check` (`agent_tools.execute_tool()`) once one isn't.
  - **Caveats, not yet resolved:** only `chat_call.py` has been updated to this contract (returns a tool message dict, e.g. `{"type": "text", "text": "..."}`, never actually `call_request` - it has no handoff decision logic of its own yet) - `architect_call.py`/`coder_call.py`/`review_call.py`/`documentation_call.py` still return the old `str | None` and would break if any `call_request` ever actually named one of them. Not reachable today since nothing produces such a request yet - flagged, not fixed, same as the equivalent gap recorded below.
  - **What's still missing for this to be a real rotation, not just plumbing:** nothing yet decides *when* a Call should emit `call_request` instead of a tool message - that's still tied to Phase 5's real LLM-decided output (a Call's LLM would need to actually choose between "hand off" and "reply" itself). The loop/signal exist and are exercised on every turn today (trivially - Chat never rotates), but the interesting case (an actual handoff) has no producer yet.
  - `call_router.py::handoff_call()` itself remains unused - `turn_handler.py` calls `call_router.get_call()` directly instead, since it already needs the module reference to call `.handle()` itself inside its own loop/error-handling, rather than through `handoff_call()`'s own thinner wrapper.
- [x] **Phase 4 — wire into `SessionWorker` - implemented 2026-09-12, per explicit instruction ("implement the tool to call thread"), scoped to Chat-only (Phase 3's handoff logic deliberately not built alongside it - see above).** `utils_session/session_worker.py::_process_batch()`'s former log-only placeholder now calls `utils_calls/turn_handler.py::run_turn_and_reply()`, which:
  - Runs `run_turn(prompt)` (same file) via `asyncio.run()` - `run_turn()` itself unconditionally calls `chat_call.handle()` and converts a raised exception or a `None` reply into an error outcome rather than propagating, so a Chat Call/`query_llm()` failure never crashes the calling thread.
  - Publishes the outcome via `utils_agents/agent_tools.py::execute_tool()`, using the caller's own `RabbitMQPublisher` (a `SessionWorker`'s long-lived `self._publisher`, passed in as an argument) - a reply as `"text"` followed by `"completed"` (a plain `"text"` send does not close a task_id on `telegram_gateway`'s side - only `"completed"`/`"error"` do, per its README), a pipeline failure as `"error"` directly, and a reply that itself fails `execute_tool()`'s own validation/publish falling back to `"error"` too, so `task_id` is never left silently open just because the reply itself was malformed/undeliverable.
  - Calls `mark_task_complete(task_id)` on every path that actually closes `task_id` out on `telegram_gateway`'s side (a successful `"completed"` or `"error"` publish) - **this is what wires up §3's previously-open "not yet wired for a batch's final `task_id`" crash-recovery gap**, closing that loop for the Chat-only path now that one exists to close it from.
  - A further, defensive `try`/`except` wraps the `asyncio.run(run_turn(...))` call itself in `run_turn_and_reply()`, on top of `run_turn()`'s own internal guard - added because an uncaught exception at this specific point would previously have been harmless (the old code was a single `logger.info()` call that cannot raise) but would now permanently propagate into and kill the calling thread. Judged worth the extra defensiveness precisely because of what this change turned a previously inert code path into, not applied as a blanket policy elsewhere in this file.
  - **What this phase deliberately does *not* do, left to the phases below:** no handoff to any other Call (Phase 3, see above - `chat_call` is hardcoded as the entry point, matching this phase's own "entry point always Chat" instruction), no LLM-decided tool-calling (the LLM never sees `agent_tools.py::get_tool_definitions()` at all - see the corrected bullet below for exactly what *is* decided instead, and by whom; see Phase 5 for real LLM-decided tool-calling), and no whitelist/access-tier gating of any kind (Phase 6).
  - **`README.md`/mermaid diagram updated to match** (`SessionWorker` section, its own Status line, the `SWFlow` subgraph) - the "agent Call pipeline not yet implemented" language is retired in favour of describing what's actually wired, still flagged everywhere as Chat-only/no-handoff/no-tool-calling.
  - **Revised, same day, per explicit instruction ("update run_turn to return a proper dict instead of a tuple. also move _run_turn_and_reply out of SessionWorker and move it to turn_handler.py. declutter SessionWorker"):**
    - `run_turn()` now returns `{"reply": str | None, "error_type": str | None, "error_message": str | None}` instead of a bare `(reply, error_type, error_message)` tuple - a self-describing shape at each call site (`outcome["reply"]` etc.) rather than positional unpacking, and a natural fit if this ever needs a further field later (no positional-arity break).
    - **`_run_turn_and_reply()` moved out of `SessionWorker` entirely, into `utils_calls/turn_handler.py` as a plain module-level function, renamed `run_turn_and_reply(publisher, session_id, task_id, prompt)`.** `publisher`/`session_id` are now explicit parameters (previously read off `self._publisher`/`self.session_id`) since the function no longer has a `self` to read them from - this also makes the function's own `RabbitMQPublisher`-never-shared-across-threads contract visible directly in its signature rather than implicit via `SessionWorker` instance state. `mark_task_complete`/`agent_tools` imports moved to `turn_handler.py` alongside it; `session_worker.py` no longer imports either, nor `asyncio` (its only use was inside the now-moved function).
    - **Rationale - keeps `SessionWorker` scoped to thread/inbox/lifecycle management, not Call pipeline mechanics.** `_process_batch()` is now a two-step coalesce-then-delegate: build `final_task_id`/`combined_text`, then a single call to `turn_handler.run_turn_and_reply(...)` - the actual run-and-publish logic (and everything that can meaningfully change as Phase 3/5 land) lives entirely in `utils_calls/`, alongside `run_turn()` itself, rather than split across two files/two domains.
    - **No behavioural change** - same validation, same publish/fallback/`mark_task_complete()` sequencing, same defensive `try`/`except` around the turn itself; this was a pure move-and-reshape, not a logic change.
  - **Corrected, same day, user-flagged: which reply tool (`"text"` etc.) to publish through was wrongly hardcoded in `turn_handler.py` instead of being the Call's own decision.** The original implementation above had `run_turn_and_reply()` assume every successful reply is a `"text"` tool call (`agent_tools.execute_tool(..., "text", {"text": reply})`) - the user correctly pointed out this decision belongs to `chat_call` (or whichever Call is running), not to the generic turn runner.
    - **`chat_call.py::handle()`'s return contract changed from `str | None` to `dict | None`** - now returns `{"tool_name": str, "arguments": dict}` (today always `{"tool_name": "text", "arguments": {"text": reply}}`, since Chat is deliberately tool-less beyond a plain text reply - see §6) or `None` on failure, unchanged. The Call itself decides which `agent_tools.py` tool its reply is wrapped in; `turn_handler.py` no longer contains that decision at all.
    - **`turn_handler.py::run_turn()`'s own return dict changed to match** - `"reply": str | None` replaced by `"tool_name": str | None, "arguments": dict | None`, passed through from whatever `chat_call.handle()` returned, unexamined. `run_turn_and_reply()`'s success branch now calls `agent_tools.execute_tool(publisher, task_id, session_id, tool_name, arguments)` with whatever the Call decided, rather than a hardcoded `"text"`.
    - **Rationale, why this matters beyond just "who owns one `if` branch":** the whole point of `agent_tools.py` existing as a table of tools (`poll`/`image`/`video`/`album`/`file`/`text`/`completed`/`error`) is that *which one* fits a given reply is content-dependent - a future Call able to choose e.g. `poll` vs `text` would need this decision made where the content is actually produced. Keeping `turn_handler.py` tool-agnostic means it never needs updating as more Calls/tools are added; only each Call's own `handle()` does.
    - **Known inconsistency, not yet fixed:** `architect_call.py`/`coder_call.py`/`review_call.py`/`documentation_call.py` still use the old `handle(prompt) -> str | None` contract - harmless today since `turn_handler.py` never calls any of them (Chat is the sole, hardcoded entry point - Phase 3 above), but they will need the same `dict | None` contract change before Phase 3's handoff loop can call any of them interchangeably with Chat. Not done now, to avoid touching four Call modules with no current caller purely for contract symmetry ahead of need.
    - **Still not real LLM-decided tool-calling** - `chat_call.py` itself hardcodes `"text"`, it doesn't ask its LLM to choose; that remains Phase 5's job (see below). This change only relocates *where* the (still-hardcoded) decision is made, from the generic turn runner to the Call that should own it.
  - **Corrected again, same day, user-flagged: `utils_agents/agent_tools.py` itself had become too complex ("too confusing... all the tuple") relative to what was actually asked for - rewritten from scratch to a plain mirror of `telegram_gateway/README.md`'s own documented format, per explicit instruction.**
    - **Every `_validate_*`/`tuple[dict | None, str | None]`-returning function, the `_VALIDATORS` dict-of-lambdas dispatch table, and the separate `validate_tool_arguments()` entry point are all gone.** Replaced by one private `_validate(message) -> str | None` (plain `if`/`elif` per type, no tuples, no indirection) and one public `execute_tool(publisher, task_id, session_id, message) -> str | None` - three functions total (plus `_is_valid_album_item()`), down from the original nine-plus-one-lambda-table version.
    - **The `tool_name`/`arguments` split is gone entirely - a "tool call" is now just `message`, the exact same dict shape `telegram_gateway/README.md` documents per type** (e.g. `{"type": "text", "text": "...", "buttons": [[...]] (optional)}`), minus `task_id`/`session_id` which `execute_tool()` still adds itself. This is a direct consequence of "a proper format has been defined in telegram_gateway README, use it" - no separate schema was invented; the agent's tool call and telegram_gateway's own payload are now the same object end to end, not translated between two shapes at the queue boundary.
    - **`TOOL_DEFINITIONS`'s verbose per-tool JSON-schema `parameters` blocks replaced by `TOOLS` - a flat list of `{name, description, format}`**, where `format` is the literal example string from the README (e.g. `'{"type": "poll", "question": "...", ...}'`) rather than a nested JSON-schema object - simpler for an LLM prompt to consume directly, and for a human to read without cross-referencing a schema against the README it's meant to mirror.
    - **`chat_call.py::handle()`'s return contract changed again to match** - `{"tool_name": str, "arguments": dict}` → `dict` that either **is** a tool message (`{"type": "text", "text": "..."}`) or `{"call_request": "<call_name>"}` (see Phase 3 above) - or `None` on failure, unchanged.
    - **`turn_handler.py::run_turn()`'s own return dict changed to match** - `"tool_name"/"arguments"` → a single `"message"` key, passed straight through to `agent_tools.execute_tool(publisher, task_id, session_id, message)` - referred to as **tools_check** in this section's own title, per the same instruction that introduced `call_request` (see Phase 3 above).
    - **No behavioural/validation change** - every case `_validate()` checks is the same set the old `_VALIDATORS` table checked (poll needs `question`+`options`, image/video/file need `url`, album needs valid `items`, text needs `text`, error needs `error_type`) - this was a structural simplification, not a rule change. The one thing genuinely dropped is the old `_validate_buttons()`'s per-button structural check (button rows needing non-empty `text`/`purpose`) - buttons are no longer validated at all here; a malformed button is still handled gracefully on `telegram_gateway`'s own side (a bad button is dropped, the message still sends as plain text - see `button_prompt_handler.py`), so this is a deliberate simplification, not a silently-reintroduced gap.
  - **Fixed, same day, user-flagged repeatedly across several turns before it actually landed: the reply tool was still being decided in Python, not by the LLM, despite each of the two revisions above claiming to move that decision "to the Call". Both of those revisions only moved *where in the code* `"text"` was hardcoded (`turn_handler.py` → `chat_call.py`) - neither ever asked the LLM itself to choose. This is the actual fix.**
    - **New `agent_tools.py::build_tool_prompt(prompt)`** - appends a plain-text instruction block, generated from `TOOLS` itself (name/description/format per entry), telling the LLM to respond with exactly one JSON object matching one of those formats and nothing else. One function, directly next to the `TOOLS` table it describes - not a new file/module.
    - **`chat_call.py::handle()` now sends `agent_tools.build_tool_prompt(prompt)` to the LLM (instead of the bare `prompt`), and parses the LLM's raw reply as JSON** (`json.loads`) - the returned `dict` **is** whatever the LLM produced, completely unmodified/uninspected by this function. `"text"` (or any other tool name) is never written anywhere in `chat_call.py` - the LLM's own output supplies `"type"` (or `"call_request"`) entirely. A reply that isn't valid JSON, or parses to something other than a `dict`, is treated as a pipeline failure (`None`) - same as an empty/failed LLM call - rather than silently falling back to a `"text"` wrapper, which would have reintroduced the exact bug being fixed here.
    - **This is real LLM-decided tool selection, provider-agnostically, today** - not gated behind Phase 5's native per-provider function-calling mechanism (Claude Agent SDK tools/MCP, OpenAI-compatible `functions`, etc., still unbuilt/unverified - see Phase 5 below, scope narrowed to reflect this). Works today because it asks for JSON via plain prompt instructions rather than a provider-specific tool-calling API - the trade-off is exactly what a prompt-engineered approach always costs: no per-provider structured-output guarantee, so a misbehaving LLM can still reply with prose instead of JSON (caught as a pipeline failure above, not validated/corrected).
    - **Not built here, explicitly out of scope for this fix:** the original spec's retry-to-LLM loop ("if tool is not valid, respond back to the agent on the correct format") - today, an LLM reply that parses as JSON but *fails* `agent_tools._validate()` (e.g. `{"type": "text"}` with no `text` field) still only reaches `turn_handler.py::run_turn_and_reply()`'s existing fallback, which reports it to the **user** as an `"error"`, not back to the LLM for another attempt. This was flagged and deliberately deferred earlier (see the still-open plan in "Open items, not yet decided" below) pending a decision on retry-count/cost - not forgotten, kept separate on purpose so this fix stays scoped to exactly what was asked ("let the LLM decide the type").
- [x] **Revised, 2026-09-13 — `call_request` replaced by `target_call`/`message`, and the in-loop rotation replaced by a per-session `dispatch_queue`, per explicit instruction. `turn_handler.py` renamed `call_dispatch_handler.py` directly on disk by the user before this entry; this covers updating every dependency on the old name (same treatment the `rukia_call.py` → `chat_call.py` rename above got) plus the actual mechanism change requested alongside it.**
  - **Dependency fallout from the rename, fixed first:** `utils_session/session_worker.py` still imported the now-nonexistent `turn_handler` module and called `run_turn_and_reply()` - both updated to `call_dispatch_handler`/`execute_dispatch_call()`. The rename had also left `dispatch_call()`'s own `call_type` parameter dead (`call_name` was still hardcoded to `_ENTRY_CALL_NAME` regardless of what was passed in), and `execute_dispatch_call()` calling `dispatch_call(prompt)` with only one argument - both would have broken every turn.
  - **`SessionWorker` now owns a `dispatch_queue: queue.Queue`, one per session, created in `__init__` alongside `inbox`, passed explicitly through `_process_batch()` → `execute_dispatch_call()` → `dispatch_call()` → `message_dissect()`.** Deliberately explicit, not module-level/`threading.local()` state - an earlier draft of this change used `threading.local()`, keyed implicitly by whichever OS thread happened to call it; flagged and rejected because correctness would have silently depended on `execute_dispatch_call()` always running on the same long-lived thread, with no error if that assumption were ever violated (a fresh, empty queue would just quietly appear instead). Passing it explicitly ties ownership to the `SessionWorker` instance itself, matching `self.inbox`'s own existing pattern - no locking needed either way, since a `SessionWorker` only ever processes one batch at a time on its own thread.
  - **Every hop - the turn's own entry, a handoff, or a corrective retry - is now expressed identically: a `{"target_call": "<call_name>", "prompt": "..."}` item on `dispatch_queue`.** `execute_dispatch_call()` seeds the entry hop (`{"target_call": "chat", "prompt": <turn's prompt>}`); `dispatch_call()`'s loop does nothing but pop one item, call that Call's `handle(prompt)`, and hand the result to `message_dissect()` - it no longer inspects a Call's result or decides a `call_name`/`prompt` itself at all, both are entirely `message_dissect()`'s job.
  - **New `message_dissect(dispatch_queue, target_call, result)` - the single place deciding what a Call's result actually is and driving whatever happens next**, replacing the old inline `elif "call_request" in result: call_name = result["call_request"]` branch. Three outcomes, all mutually exclusive:
    - **Unparseable/wrong shape** (not a dict, and not JSON that parses to one) - queues a corrective retry back to the *same* `target_call`, telling it what a valid reply looks like.
    - **Call pass** (`"target_call"` and `"message"` both present) - queues the handoff, using the LLM's own supplied `"message"` as the next Call's prompt (not the original turn's prompt reused verbatim, unlike the old `call_request` shape, which had no way to give the next Call anything other than the same prompt the current Call received).
    - **Tool call** (anything else) - validated for real via `agent_tools.validate_message()` (previously this branch was reached by bare elimination, with no actual validation - a genuinely malformed reply that was merely *not* a call pass would have been wrongly treated as final and published as-is). A pass queues nothing and returns the outcome dict for `dispatch_call()` to hand straight back; a failure queues a corrective retry using `validate_message()`'s own specific error text (mirroring `chat_call.py`'s own existing `"Your previous reply was invalid: {error}"` retry phrasing), not a generic fallback message.
    - Both retry cases and the handoff case return `None` (dispatch_call()'s loop continues); only a successful tool call returns non-`None` (dispatch_call()'s loop stops and returns it).
  - **`_drain(dispatch_queue)`, called only on `dispatch_call()`'s hop-limit path** - every other terminal path (unrecognised `target_call`, a raised exception, a `None` reply) returns before `message_dissect()` ever gets a chance to queue anything, so the queue is already empty on those. The hop-limit path is the one exception: its final iteration may have just queued a further hop that the loop then never consumes - left alone, since `dispatch_queue` is reused across this `SessionWorker`'s whole lifetime rather than recreated per turn, that item would silently leak into the *next* turn. `_drain()` prevents that.
  - **`agent_tools.py`: `verify_call_request()` → `verify_target_call()`**, validating `{"target_call", "message"}` instead of `{"call_request"}` - now also requires `message` to be non-empty (the old check only ever validated the call name existed). `resolve_reply()`'s branch condition changed from `"call_request" in message` to `"target_call" in message` to match. `chat_call.py`'s own Notes/docstring updated to describe the new shape - no code change needed there, since it already just forwards `resolve_reply()`'s result unexamined.
  - **New `settings.CALL_MAX_HOPS` (`config.py`), replacing the module constant `_MAX_CALL_HOPS`** - bounds the same loop as before, now counting both handoffs and corrective retries together rather than handoffs alone (a Call stuck emitting invalid replies is bounded by the same limit as a Call stuck handing off). Deliberately **not** plumbed through `compose.dev.yml`/`config_sample.ini`/`setup.sh` - it's an internal tuning knob, following the same precedent already set by `SESSION_INBOX_MAX_SIZE`/`SESSION_SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`/`REDIS_TASK_MAX_ATTEMPTS`, none of which are exposed through those either. `_ENTRY_CALL_NAME` stays a plain module constant (a fixed design decision - "entry point is always chat" - not a tunable).
  - **What this still doesn't do:** `architect_call.py`/`coder_call.py`/`review_call.py`/`documentation_call.py` are still on the old `str | None` contract (same known gap as before, just carried forward under the new name) - a `target_call` naming any of them would still break today, not reachable in practice since nothing produces a call pass yet.
- [x] **Resolved, same day, per explicit instruction ("chat_call is invalid, update it to follow other call.py. and clean up agent_tools.py"): the "two retry mechanisms stacked" item immediately above is resolved by removing `chat_call.py`'s own one, not `message_dissect()`'s.**
  - **`chat_call.py::handle()` stripped back to the exact same shape every other `<name>_call.py` already uses** - one `if`/`else`, a single `query_llm()` call, no loop, no `_MAX_FORMAT_ATTEMPTS` constant. Return contract changed from `dict | None` back to `str | None` - the LLM's raw reply, entirely unexamined, same as `architect_call.py`/`coder_call.py`/`review_call.py`/`documentation_call.py`'s own `handle()`. The one difference from those four: `agent_tools.build_tool_prompt(prompt)` still wraps the prompt before it goes to `query_llm()`, since Chat is still the one Call whose reply has to resolve to a tool call or a call pass - the other four don't have that contract yet (§5's own still-open item, unchanged by this).
  - **`agent_tools.py::resolve_reply()` and `verify_target_call()` deleted outright** - both had exactly one caller (`chat_call.py`'s now-removed retry loop) and are fully superseded by `message_dissect()`'s own parsing/shape-check/`validate_message()` call, one layer up in `call_dispatch_handler.py`. The `json` import (only ever used by `resolve_reply()`) removed alongside them. `execute_tool()`'s own `validate_message()` call is **not** redundant in the same way and was left alone - it's still the only check ever run against a `{"type": "error"}`/`{"type": "completed"}` payload, which `execute_dispatch_call()` builds directly in Python and never routes through `message_dissect()` at all.
  - **Net effect:** every Call's reply is now validated/corrected in exactly one place (`message_dissect()`, call-agnostic), instead of `chat_call.py` doing its own pass first and `message_dissect()` doing a second, redundant one behind it.
- [x] **Fixed, same day, user-reported: a poll was being closed with "completed" immediately after being sent, instead of being left open awaiting the responder's answer.** `execute_dispatch_call()` previously followed *every* successful `execute_tool()` publish with an unconditional `"completed"`, with no exception for `poll`.
  - **Per `telegram_gateway/README.md`'s own "Poll answers"/`poll_timed_out` sections: a poll's `task_id` is routed back to "whichever agent currently owns that task" once it concludes (a real `poll_answer` or, if unanswered, a `poll_timed_out` push) - "the decision belongs to that agent... not to this gateway."** Closing `task_id` with `"completed"` the moment the poll itself was sent contradicted that contract outright - telegram_gateway would still faithfully route the eventual answer back to a `task_id` bot_sanctuary had already considered finished.
  - **`execute_dispatch_call()` now special-cases `message.get("type") == "poll"`**: after a successful publish, neither `"completed"` nor `mark_task_complete(task_id)` are called - `task_id` is deliberately left open, on both telegram_gateway's side and bot_sanctuary's own crash-recovery tracking, until whatever later turn actually handles the eventual `poll_answer`/`poll_timed_out` closes it out for real.
  - **Related gap noticed while verifying this against the gateway's contract, not fixed here:** `SessionWorker._process_batch()`'s `combined_text` is built purely from each queued item's `"text"` field - a `poll_answer` push's actual answer (`"poll_answer": [0, 2]`, per the Task Queue Payload) is never read at all, so the eventual turn handling a poll response currently has no way to see what was actually answered, only whatever (typically empty) `"text"` accompanied it. Flagging only - this is a `SessionWorker`/coalescing-side gap, separate from the poll-closing fix above, and wasn't part of what was asked.
  - **Same fix extended, 2026-09-16, user-reported: a `text` reply carrying `buttons` had the identical premature-`completed` bug** - "complete is sent despite sending a button... similar issue to polling previously." Investigated first against the full round trip on both sides (`telegram_gateway`'s registration/validation of a button press, session-reset handling, restart behaviour) before settling on scope, per an initial over-broad draft plan (poll-parity press-routing/expiry/Redis machinery) that was explicitly rejected: *"button has been planned out, stop with new ideas and flaws... it is similar to poll but it is not a poll."* **Final, much narrower scope, confirmed directly: the button feature itself (send/register/validate a press) is already considered complete; only the completion-timing rule needed fixing** - the exact same one-line shape as the poll fix above, nothing else.
    - **`execute_dispatch_call()`'s existing `elif message.get("type") == "poll":` broadened to `elif message.get("type") == "poll" or (message.get("type") == "text" and message.get("buttons")):`** - identical treatment either way (publish, skip `execute_completed()`/`mark_task_complete()`, log it). No new module, no press-routing event, no expiry timer, no session-reset parity code, no Redis - all explicitly ruled out, not merely deferred.
    - **Deliberately not a cross-`task_id` tracking mechanism** - per direct instruction ("it should close on the next send unless it is still a button"), each task_id's own completion decision is made independently, purely from its own reply's own `type`/`buttons` fields, the same way poll's own check already worked - there is no mechanism (and none was asked for) that reaches back to close an *earlier* still-open buttons/poll task_id from a *later* turn.
    - **Restart behaviour, explicitly decided by the user rather than assumed:** *"let it restart, just do not close that task_id. If telegram_gateway restarts then let it lose it. If bot_sanctuary restarts then it's just an orphan new message and task_id will be closed."* A task_id left open by this fix is not given any special crash-recovery treatment beyond what already exists generically for any other orphaned task_id - consistent with the narrow scope above.
    - **Docstring/inline comments updated to describe both cases together** (`execute_dispatch_call()`'s own Notes, and the branch's own inline comment) - the poll case's existing wording is preserved, a buttons-specific sentence added alongside it, not rewritten.
- [ ] **Phase 5 — native per-provider tool-calling, and filesystem/code tool access per Call (§6).** Narrowed (2026-09-12) now that LLM-decided reply-tool selection is handled provider-agnostically via prompt-engineered JSON (see the fix above) - what remains here is a provider's own *native* tool-calling mechanism (Claude Agent SDK tool definitions/MCP if `LLM_<CALL>_TYPE` is `claude`; open question for any non-Claude provider), which would replace the JSON-prompt approach with a real structured-output guarantee, plus wiring filesystem/code tool access into Architect/Coder/Review/Documentation only - Chat stays deliberately tool-less on that front per §6 (aside from `PERSONA_PROMPT`'s own stated web-search access, not yet actually wired to any tool - see chat_call.py's own Notes).
- [ ] **Phase 6 — whitelist/access-tier enforcement.** Read the access-tier field `telegram_gateway` stamps on the inbound task payload (exact shape still an open item, see "Open items" below) and restrict a non-whitelisted task to Chat with no handoff permitted at all, per this section's own requirement above.
- [ ] **Phase 7 — observability/testing.** Structured logging for a handoff chain (which Calls a turn passed through, not just the final reply), and a manual test path (mirroring `utils_smtp/smtp_handler.py`'s `python -m ...` pattern) to exercise a Call/handoff without needing the full RabbitMQ pipeline running.

### Claude session service lifecycle — `initialise_llm_services()`/`terminate_llm_services()` (`agent_interface.py`) + `initialise_claude()`/`terminate_claude()` (`claude_interface.py`) — implemented 2026-09-14

**Context, and a documentation gap flagged rather than silently filled in.** `utils_agents/services/claude_session_service.py` (an always-on platform holding one persistent `ClaudeSDKClient` per generation directory, for genuine cross-turn continuity/prompt-cache eligibility - see its own extensive module header) and the `utils_agents/` restructuring into `interfaces/`/`services/` subdirectories both already exist on disk, dated 2026-09-13 per the former's own header, but **neither was ever recorded in this file** before this entry. This turn's own instruction was narrowly "create an initialise and de-initialise interface in agent_interface to start and stop claude" - it does not retroactively document that prior, separate body of work, which remains an open gap in this file. What follows covers only what this turn actually touched.

- [x] **New `agent_interface.py::initialise_claude()`/`terminate_claude()`** - the requested start/stop pair, named to match this codebase's existing `initialise_<thing>()`/`terminate_<thing>()` convention (`utilities/initialise.py::initialise_application()`/`terminate_application()`), rather than `start_*`/`stop_*` (`claude_session_service.py`'s own internal pair, or `session_worker.py::start_session_reset_schedule()`/`stop_session_reset_schedule()`) - chosen since these two sit at the same "application-facing lifecycle" layer as `initialise_application()`/`terminate_application()`, one level above the `start_claude_session_service()`/`stop_claude_session_service()` pair they call into.
- [x] **OAuth-gated, per explicit instruction ("claude can only be started if claude is authenticated by OAUTH").** `initialise_claude()` only calls `start_claude_session_service()` if `settings.LLM_CLAUDE_ACCESS_TYPE.upper() == "OAUTH"` **and** `settings.LLM_CLAUDE_TOKEN` is non-empty - an API-key-configured Claude (`LLM_CLAUDE_ACCESS_TYPE="API"`), or no Claude credential configured at all, is a no-op (logged at info), not an error. `terminate_claude()` has no equivalent guard - it calls `stop_claude_session_service()` unconditionally, since that function is already its own no-op if the service was never started (see its own docstring).
- [x] **Credential bridging moved here, deliberately, not left inside `claude_session_service.py` itself.** `claude_session_service.py`'s `ClaudeSDKClient` has no per-call credential parameter the way `claude_interface.py::query_via_oauth()`/`query_via_api()` do (both bridge their own `token` argument into `os.environ` right before their own SDK call) - its own module header already flags this as an inherited, unresolved constraint ("Credential setup ... still mutates process-wide os.environ ... unchanged by this module's own single-Claude-credential-configured-at-once reality today"). `initialise_claude()` is now the one place that sets `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` from `settings.LLM_CLAUDE_TOKEN`, once, immediately before `start_claude_session_service()` - not a new mechanism, the same env-var-mutation approach `claude_interface.py` already uses, just relocated to the one call site that actually needs it for this platform.
- [x] **Fixed in passing, necessarily, not a style choice: `agent_interface.py`'s own provider imports were stale and would have raised `ImportError` at import time.** `from . import claude_interface`/`codex_interface`/`deepseek_interface`/`qwen_interface` still pointed at `utils_agents/` directly - all four modules now live under `utils_agents/interfaces/` (part of the undocumented restructuring flagged above). Corrected to `from .interfaces import claude_interface` etc., and the new `claude_session_service` pair imported via `from .services.claude_session_service import start_claude_session_service, stop_claude_session_service`. Not optional/deferrable - `agent_interface.py` could not have been imported at all (by anything, including this turn's own new functions) without this fix.
- [x] **Corrected, same day, user-flagged: renamed `initialise_claude()`/`terminate_claude()` → `initialise_llm_services()`/`terminate_llm_services()`, per explicit instruction ("agent_interface.py should not have a function that solely initialise claude. whatever agent_interface hold should be for all LLM").** `agent_interface.py` is this codebase's one deliberately provider-agnostic module (`query_llm()`/`load_persona()`/`test_llm_tokens()` all dispatch across all four providers already, never named after one) - a Claude-named top-level function broke that pattern. Behaviour is unchanged (still exactly one branch, Claude's own OAuth-gated `claude_session_service.py` start/stop, still the only provider with an always-on platform to start), only the function names/docstrings changed to frame this as "the provider-agnostic lifecycle entry point, which today only has a Claude branch" rather than "a Claude-specific function". A future provider's own always-on platform (if one is ever built) would add a further branch inside these same two functions, not a new sibling top-level function named after it.
- [x] **Corrected again, same day, per explicit instruction ("create initialise_claude and terminate_claude in claude_interface.py these two function will be called in agent_interface. claude_interface will determine which to be initialised depending on whether it is accessed by OAUTH or API"): the OAuth-gating/credential-bridging logic just landed inside `agent_interface.py`'s own `initialise_llm_services()`/`terminate_llm_services()` (previous bullet) is moved back out again, into a new `claude_interface.py::initialise_claude()`/`terminate_claude()` pair - `agent_interface.py`'s two functions are now pure delegation (`claude_interface.initialise_claude()`/`claude_interface.terminate_claude()`, nothing else).**
  - **Rationale: this is a different, more precise fix than simply renaming (previous bullet) - it relocates the actual decision, not just its name.** `agent_interface.py` staying provider-agnostic no longer relies on "the Claude branch is well-commented" (the previous correction's approach) - it now genuinely contains zero Claude-specific logic; every provider-specific decision (does *this* provider have an always-on platform, and under what precondition should it start) lives entirely inside that provider's own `<provider>_interface.py`, matching `query_llm()`'s own existing "each provider module owns its own behaviour" shape.
  - **Deliberate, explicitly-instructed exception to this module's own existing convention: `initialise_claude()`/`terminate_claude()` read `settings.LLM_CLAUDE_ACCESS_TYPE`/`settings.LLM_CLAUDE_TOKEN` directly, rather than taking them as parameters.** This is the opposite of `query_via_oauth()`/`query_via_api()`'s own settled shape one section up (§2's "per-provider credentials" entry - both take `token` as an explicit argument specifically so no `<provider>_interface.py` needs its own `from ...config import settings` import, with `agent_interface.py` as "the sole place that resolves config → concrete credential"). Per explicit instruction ("these variables should be retrieved from config and not passed in"), `claude_interface.py` now re-gains a `from ....config import settings` import for these two functions only. **Superseded a moment later, same day - see the next bullet: `query_via_oauth()`/`query_via_api()`'s own `token` argument stopped being read at all shortly after this bullet was written**, so the "only these two functions" framing above no longer describes the whole file - `settings` is still only read by `initialise_claude()`/`terminate_claude()` (unchanged), but `token` itself is now unread anywhere in this file.
  - **`claude_interface.py` also gains `from ..services.claude_session_service import start_claude_session_service, stop_claude_session_service`** - previously imported by `agent_interface.py` itself (now removed from there, along with its now-unused `os` import - credential bridging moved with the logic that needed it).
  - **No behavioural change from the previous bullet's end state** - same OAuth-only gate, same `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` bridging, same underlying `start_claude_session_service()`/`stop_claude_session_service()` calls - only *which file* contains the decision and the config read changed.
- [x] **Revised, same day, per explicit instruction ("begin the implementation to run once on startup. and log selection choice. if none, assume unknown decision, log it and do nothing. test will only come after initialise_llm is made"): credential bridging moved from every `query_via_oauth()`/`query_via_api()` call to exactly once, inside `initialise_claude()`, per this project's own earlier flagged discussion of the issues this would create (see the "suggest issues that might arise" review two turns prior - not reproduced here, but this instruction is the user's decision to proceed despite them).**
  - **`initialise_claude()` rewritten to three mutually-exclusive branches, each logged distinctly** (`settings.LLM_CLAUDE_ACCESS_TYPE.strip().upper()` resolved once): `"OAUTH"` + a configured token → bridges `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`, starts `claude_session_service.py`; `"API"` + a configured token → bridges `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` only (no persistent platform for this access type - the bridge exists purely for `query_via_api()`'s own one-shot calls); anything else (unset/unrecognised access type, or its own token missing) → **per explicit instruction ("if none, assume unknown decision, log it and do nothing")** - logged as an unresolved/unknown configuration decision, no environment mutation, no service start. Every branch logs at info, including the "unknown decision" one, so an operator can distinguish all three outcomes from the logs alone, not just the two "something happened" ones.
  - **`query_via_oauth()`/`query_via_api()`'s own `os.environ[...] = token` lines removed outright** - both now do nothing but call `_run_query()`. **Superseded a moment later, same day - see the next bullet: `query_via_oauth()` no longer unconditionally calls `_run_query()` either.** `token` remains in both signatures, accepted and unread, purely for call-signature parity with the other three providers' own `query_via_oauth()`/`query_via_api()` (`agent_interface.py::query_llm()` calls all four identically) - the same "accepted-and-ignored for uniformity" precedent already used elsewhere in this codebase (e.g. `cwd` on `codex_interface.py`/`deepseek_interface.py`/`qwen_interface.py`).
  - **Accepted, explicitly-flagged consequence, not fixed by this turn: `query_via_oauth()`/`query_via_api()` now depend on `initialise_claude()` having already run with the same access type still in effect.** Calling either beforehand, or after Claude's configuration has since changed to a different access type, now sends an unauthenticated request rather than one authenticated by that call's own `token` argument - previously each call was self-sufficient regardless of startup ordering. This was raised as a specific risk before this instruction was given (see the prior "suggest issues" review) and knowingly accepted, not overlooked.
  - **Explicitly deferred, per the instruction's own final sentence ("test will only come after initialise_llm is made"): `test_llm_tokens()`'s own startup smoke test is not yet resequenced to run after `initialise_llm_services()`.** This is the most immediate concrete instance of the ordering risk directly above - `test_llm_tokens()` calling `query_via_api()` for a Claude/API deployment before `initialise_llm_services()`/`initialise_claude()` has ever run would currently fail authentication. Left open on purpose, per the user's own stated sequencing (this function first, the test-ordering fix afterward) - not wired into `initialise.py` at all yet regardless (see the still-open "Not wired into `utilities/initialise.py`" item below).
- [x] **Further revised, same day, per explicit instruction ("update query_via_oauth to use claude_session_service.py"): `query_via_oauth()` now routes through `claude_session_service.py::query_via_service()` - this project's genuinely persistent, per-generation Claude platform - instead of unconditionally calling this module's own one-shot `_run_query()`.**
  - **New branch: `if session_dir is not None: return query_via_service(session_dir, prompt)`, else falls back to the existing `_run_query(prompt, persona, session_dir)`.** `session_dir` is `None` only for `agent_interface.py`'s own startup credential smoke test (`_send_llm_test_prompt()` calls `query_llm()` with no `session_dir` at all) - every real Chat Call turn already carries one (`SessionWorker.session_dir`, threaded through unconditionally), so in practice this makes the persistent-platform path the one every live OAuth conversation actually takes, with the one-shot path now reduced to a smoke-test-only fallback for this access type.
  - **`query_via_api()` deliberately left untouched** - `claude_session_service.py`'s platform is OAuth-only by its own `initialise_claude()` gate (no persistent client is ever started for `access_type="API"`), so there is nothing for `query_via_api()` to route to; it still always calls `_run_query()`.
  - **Real, explicitly-flagged behavioural divergence: `persona` is silently unused on the `query_via_service()` path.** That platform loads its own persona/tools/model from `libraries/claude/chat.json` via its own `_parse_agent()` (a different file, a different format, from `agent_interface.py::load_persona()`'s `libraries/<llm_type>/<call_name>.md`) - never from whatever string this function was actually called with. A caller's `persona` argument now only takes effect when `session_dir` is `None` (the smoke test) - for every real turn, whatever is written in `libraries/claude/chat.json` wins regardless of what `chat_call.py` loaded from `libraries/claude/chat.md` and passed down. Not reconciled by this change - flagged as a pre-existing design split between the two persona-loading mechanisms, now made load-bearing rather than latent.
  - **Related, pre-existing constraint also newly load-bearing: `claude_session_service.py`'s persona file is hardcoded Chat-only** (`_LLM_TYPE="claude"`, filename fixed to `"chat.json"`, no `call_name` parameter at all). Harmless today since Chat is the only Call actually reachable end-to-end (§5's Phase 3/4 history - Architect/Coder/Review/Documentation aren't wired to a real caller yet), but if any of those four ever does resolve to `llm_type="claude"`+`access_type="OAUTH"` with its own `session_dir`, it would receive the Chat Call's persona, not its own - a real gap for a future revisit, not addressed here.
  - **`query_via_service()` is a blocking, synchronous function** (internally waits on a `concurrent.futures.Future`, up to `settings.AGENT_QUERY_TIMEOUT_SECONDS`) **called directly (not awaited) from inside this `async def` function.** Judged consistent with this codebase's existing blocking-call precedent rather than a new risk - this function's own caller already runs one turn at a time on one `SessionWorker`'s dedicated thread (`asyncio.run()` per turn, nothing else scheduled on that same loop concurrently), so blocking that thread for a reply is no different in practice from `_run_query()`'s own already-blocking-in-effect behaviour on the same call path.
  - **Inherits, rather than introduces, the ordering dependency already recorded above:** this path does nothing at all (`query_via_service()` itself logs an error and returns `None`) unless `claude_session_service.py`'s background thread/loop is already running (`start_claude_session_service()`, called from `initialise_claude()`) - still not wired into `initialise.py` (see the still-open item below), so this path is entirely untested end-to-end as of this entry.
- [x] **Final cleanup, same day, per explicit instruction ("clean up unused token parameter in query within claude_interface.py"): `query_via_oauth()`/`query_via_api()` no longer accept `token` at all - the "accepted and unread, kept for signature parity" compromise from two revisions back is retired, since the parameter was genuinely dead (never read anywhere in either function's own body once credential bridging moved to `initialise_claude()`), not merely unused-but-harmless.**
  - **`claude_interface.py::query_via_oauth(prompt, persona, session_dir)`/`query_via_api(prompt, persona, session_dir)`** - both lost their `token: str` parameter and its `Args` docstring entry outright.
  - **This is now the one place `claude_interface.py`'s own call signature diverges from `codex_interface.py`/`deepseek_interface.py`/`qwen_interface.py`'s otherwise-uniform `query_via_oauth(prompt, token, persona, session_dir)`/`query_via_api(...)` shape** - those three still bridge their own credential per call (no startup-time equivalent to `initialise_claude()` exists for any of them), so `token` is still genuinely used there; only Claude's has become dead.
  - **`agent_interface.py::query_llm()` updated to match - a new `if llm_type == "claude":` branch inside both the `OAUTH` and `API` arms, calling `claude_interface`'s two functions without `token`, while `codex`/`deepseek`/`qwen` keep the original four-argument call.** This is a deliberate, acknowledged reintroduction of a per-provider branch into what this module's own header otherwise describes as "provider-agnostic" - flagged rather than hidden, since removing a genuinely dead parameter from `claude_interface.py` necessarily means `agent_interface.py` can no longer call all four providers with one identical line. Judged the honest trade-off: keeping `token` in `claude_interface.py`'s signature purely to preserve dispatch uniformity would have kept a parameter that lies about what the function actually does, which is what this instruction asked to stop.
  - **No behavioural change** - `token` was already unread by either Claude function before this cleanup (see the two revisions above); this only removes the now-misleading parameter and updates the one call site that supplied it.
- [x] **Wired into `utilities/initialise.py`, same day, per explicit instruction ("implement the initialise and termination of agent_interface.py to application"): `initialise_application()`/`terminate_application()` now call `initialise_llm_services()`/`terminate_llm_services()` - the two items immediately below are both resolved by this.**
  - ~~Not wired into `utilities/initialise.py`.~~ **Resolved.** `initialise_application()` now calls `initialise_llm_services()` as its very first step, ahead of `test_llm_tokens()`; `terminate_application()` calls `terminate_llm_services()` after `shutdown_all_session_workers()`, ahead of `close_rabbitmq_connection()`.
  - ~~`test_llm_tokens()` ordering relative to `initialise_llm_services()` remains unresolved.~~ **Resolved, per the same instruction's own earlier stated plan ("test will only come after initialise_llm is made" - see two revisions above).** `initialise_llm_services()` now runs before `test_llm_tokens()`, closing the ordering risk flagged in every revision above (Claude/OAuth's smoke test would otherwise run before its own credential was ever bridged).
  - **Shutdown ordering deliberately placed `terminate_llm_services()` *after* `shutdown_all_session_workers()`, not before** - a `SessionWorker` finishing its last queued turn may still be mid-call against Claude's persistent session platform (`claude_session_service.py::query_via_service()`, itself a blocking call - see the "update query_via_oauth" revision above); stopping the platform first would risk disconnecting a client still genuinely in use. Placed ahead of `close_rabbitmq_connection()`/`close_redis_connection()`, matching this function's existing "drain work before tearing down connections" ordering for everything else in it.
  - **`README.md` updated to match** - both the prose Startup/Shutdown step lists and the mermaid diagram (`Project Architecture`) gained the new `initialise_llm_services()`/`terminate_llm_services()` steps, at the same two points described above.
